The slope of the line can be found using the tangent of the given angle.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Formula:** The slope \( m \) of a line is equal to the tangent of its inclination angle \( θ \). So, \( m = \tan(θ) \).

2. **Plug in the Angle:** Here, \( θ = 120° \), so \( m = \tan(120°) \).

3. **Use Trigonometric Identity:** Knowing that \( 120° \) is equivalent to \( 180° - 60° \), we can use the identity for tangent:
   \[
   \tan(180° - θ) = -\tan(θ)
   \]
   Thus, \( \tan(120°) = -\tan(60°) \).

4. **Calculate \( \tan(60°) \):** From trigonometric values,
   \[
   \tan(60°) = \sqrt{3}
   \]

5. **Find the Slope:** Substituting back, we get:
   \[
   m = -\sqrt{3}
   \]

The slope of the line is \( -\sqrt{3} \).

***********************<think>
First, I recognize that when given two lines with an angle between them and the slope of one line, we can use the formula involving the tangent of the angle to find the slope of the other line.

The formula for the angle \(\theta\) between two lines with slopes \(m_1\) and \(m_2\) is:

\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot m_2} \right|
\]

Given:
- The angle \(\theta = \frac{\pi}{4}\), so \(\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 1\)
- One slope \(m_1 = \frac{1}{4}\)

Plugging these into the formula:

\[
1 = \left| \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2} \right|
\]

This absolute value equation results in two possible equations:

1. \(1 = \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4}m_2}\)
2. \(1 = -\left(\frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4}m_2}\right)\)

Solving both equations for \(m_2\) will give the possible slopes of the second line.
</think>

To determine the slope (\(m_2\)) of a second line that forms an angle of \(\frac{\pi}{4}\) with another line having a slope of \(\frac{1}{4}\), we'll use the formula for the tangent of the angle between two lines:

\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot m_2} \right|
\]

**Given:**
- Angle, \(\theta = \frac{\pi}{4}\), so \(\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 1\)
- Slope of the first line, \(m_1 = \frac{1}{4}\)

Plugging these values into the formula:

\[
1 = \left| \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} \right|
\]

This absolute value equation splits into two scenarios:

1. **Positive Case:**
   \[
   1 = \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2}
   \]
   
   Solving for \(m_2\):
   \[
   1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2 = m_2 - \frac{1}{4} \\
   1 + \frac{1}{4} = m_2 - \frac{1}{4} m_2 \\
   1 + \frac{1}{4} = \frac{3}{4} m_2 \\
   \frac{5}{4} = \frac{3}{4} m_2 \\
   m_2 = \frac{5}{4} \cdot \frac{4}{3} = \frac{5}{3}
   \]

2. **Negative Case:**
   \[
   1 = -\left( \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} \right)
   \]
   
   Solving for \(m_2\):
   \[
   -1 = \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} \\
   -(1) \cdot (1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2) = m_2 - \frac{1}{4} \\
   -1 - \frac{1}{4} m_2 = m_2 - \frac{1}{4} \\
   -1 + \frac{1}{4} = m_2 + \frac{1}{4} m_2 \\
   -\frac{3}{4} = \frac{5}{4} m_2 \\
   m_2 = -\frac{3}{4} \cdot \frac{4}{5} = -\frac{3}{5}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The possible slopes of the second line are:

\[
m_2 = \boxed{\frac{5}{3}} \quad \text{or} \quad m_2 = \boxed{-\frac{3}{5}}
\]

   - P1: (-6,7)
   - P2: (2,9)
   - P3: (7,-3)
   - P4: (-2,0)

2. **Apply Shoelace Formula**:
   The formula is Area = (1/2)|sum over i of (x_i * y_{i+1} - x_{i+1} * y_i)|

3. **Calculate Each Term**:
   - First sum: x_i * y_{i+1}
     - (-6)*9 = -54
     - 2*(-3) = -6
     - 7*0 = 0
     - (-2)*7 = -14
     Total = -54 + (-6) + 0 + (-14) = -74
   
   - Second sum: y_i * x_{i+1}
     - 7*2 = 14
     - 9*7 = 63
     - (-3)*(-2) = 6
     - 0*(-6) = 0

     - (2)*7 = 14
     - (-6)*0 = 0
     - (-2)*(-3)=6
     - 7*9=63
     Total = 83

     - 9*(-6)=-54
     - 7*(-2)=-14
     - 0*7=0
     - (-3)*2=-6
     Total = -74

4. **Compute the Difference**:
   - Difference = |83 - (-74)| = |157| = 157

***********************<think>
First, I need to find a point on the x-axis that is equidistant from the points (9, 8) and (5, 6).

Since any point on the x-axis has coordinates (x, 0), let's denote our desired point as P(x, 0).

Next, I'll calculate the distance from P to both given points using the distance formula.

The distance from P to (9, 8) is √[(9 - x)² + (8 - 0)²] = √[(9 - x)² + 64].

Similarly, the distance from P to (5, 6) is √[(5 - x)² + (6 - 0)²] = √[(5 - x)² + 36].

Since these distances are equal, I can set up the equation:

√[(9 - x)² + 64] = √[(5 - x)² + 36]

To eliminate the square roots, I'll square both sides of the equation:

(9 - x)² + 64 = (5 - x)² + 36

Expanding both sides gives:

81 - 18x + x² + 64 = 25 - 10x + x² + 36

Combining like terms simplifies to:

-8x + 145 = -10x + 61

Solving for x, I bring all terms to one side:

2x = -84 ⇒ x = -42

Therefore, the point on the x-axis that is equidistant from (9, 8) and (5, 6) is (-42, 0).
</think>

**Solution:**

We need to find a point \((x, 0)\) on the \(x\)-axis that is equidistant from the points \((9, 8)\) and \((5, 6)\).

1. **Distance from \((x, 0)\) to \((9, 8)\):**
   \[
   d_1 = \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64}
   \]

2. **Distance from \((x, 0)\) to \((5, 6)\):**
   \[
   d_2 = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36}
   \]

3. **Set the distances equal and solve for \(x\):**
   \[
   \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36}
   \]
   
   Squaring both sides:
   \[
   (9 - x)^2 + 64 = (5 - x)^2 + 36
   \]
   
   Expanding and simplifying:
   \[
   81 - 18x + x^2 + 64 = 25 - 10x + x^2 + 36
   \]
   \[
   -8x + 145 = -10x + 61
   \]
   
   Solving for \(x\):
   \[
   2x = -84 \\
   x = -42
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{-42}

Point A: (4,4)
Point B: (3,5)
Point C: (-1,-1)

Now, I'll calculate the slope between each pair of points.

Starting with AB. The slope formula is (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1). So for points A(4,4) and B(3,5):
Slope of AB = (5 - 4)/(3 - 4) = 1/(-1) = -1

Next, let's find the slope between BC. Points B(3,5) and C(-1,-1):
Slope of BC = (-1 - 5)/(-1 - 3) = (-6)/(-4) = 3/2

Lastly, I'll calculate the slope between AC. Points A(4,4) and C(-1,-1):
Slope of AC = (-1 - 4)/(-1 - 4) = (-5)/(-5) = 1

Finally, checking AB and AC:
Slope AB is -1, slope AC is 1. Multiplying: (-1)*(1) = -1
Ah, that's exactly what we're looking for! A product of -1 means these two sides are perpendicular.

Therefore, the triangle formed by points (4,4), (3,5), and (-1,-1) is indeed a right-angled triangle because the slopes of AB and AC indicate a right angle at point A or C. Since both points have positive coordinates except for one, but since we've already found two sides with slopes that multiply to -1, it's confirmed.

Since the line makes a 60-degree angle upwards from the positive y-axis towards the left (negative x-direction), its angle with the positive x-axis is 180 - 60 = 120 degrees.

Next, I know that the angle θ₂ for this line can be found using the arctangent function because the slope is equal to the tangent of the angle it makes with the x-axis. So θ₂ = arctan(m) = arctan(-1). Since tan(-45°) is -1, that means θ₂ is -45 degrees. Wait, no, because when dealing with slopes, if the slope is negative, the angle is actually in the second quadrant. So θ₂ would be 180° - 45° = 135° from the positive x-axis.

But wait, sometimes people take the absolute value of the arctangent result to get a positive acute angle between the line and the x-axis. So perhaps it's better to consider just the magnitude here because we're interested in the angle between two lines regardless of their direction. The formula for the angle φ between two lines with slopes m₁ and m₂ is given by tanφ = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁m₂)|.

In this case, since one slope is 0 (the x-axis), it simplifies to tanφ = |(m₂ - 0)/(1 + 0*m₂)| = |m₂|. So φ = arctan(|m₂|) which is arctan(1) because m₂ is -1. Arctan(1) is 45°, so the angle between them should be 45 degrees.

Wait a second, but earlier I thought θ₂ was 135°, and since the x-axis is at 0°, the difference would be 135° - 0° = 135°. But that seems conflicting with the previous result. Hmm, maybe I'm mixing up the concepts here.

Let me clarify: when calculating the angle between two lines, we consider the smallest angle between them. So even if one line is in the second quadrant (like 135°), the angle between it and the x-axis would be measured as the acute angle, which is 45°, not 135°.

So using the formula tanφ = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁m₂)| where m₁=0 and m₂=-1, we get tanφ = |(-1 - 0)/(1 + 0)| = |-1|/1 = 1. So φ = arctan(1) = 45°, which is the correct acute angle between the two lines.

Alright, so I have this problem where there are two straight lines. The slope of one line is twice the slope of the other. Also, the tangent of the angle between them is 1/3. I need to find the slopes of these two lines.

Hmm, okay, let me recall some formulas. I remember that if we have two lines with slopes m1 and m2, the tangent of the angle (θ) between them is given by:

tan(θ) = |(m2 - m1)/(1 + m1*m2)|

In this problem, it's mentioned that tan(θ) is 1/3. Also, one slope is twice the other, so let me denote the slopes as m and 2m.

So substituting into the formula:

tan(θ) = |(2m - m)/(1 + m*(2m))| = |m/(1 + 2m²)|

We know tan(θ) is 1/3, so setting them equal:

|m/(1 + 2m²)| = 1/3

Since the absolute value can be removed by considering both positive and negative cases, but let's square both sides to eliminate the absolute value and make it easier:

(m)^2 / (1 + 2m²)^2 = (1/3)^2
=> m^2 / (1 + 2m²)^2 = 1/9

Cross-multiplying:

9m^2 = (1 + 2m²)^2

Expanding the right side:

(1 + 2m²)^2 = 1 + 4m² + 4m^4

So now equation becomes:

9m^2 = 1 + 4m² + 4m^4

Let me bring all terms to one side:

0 = 4m^4 + 4m² + 1 - 9m²
Simplify the terms:

4m^4 -5m² +1=0

So, we have a quadratic in terms of m². Let's let u = m².

Then equation becomes:

4u² -5u +1=0

Now, solving for u using quadratic formula:

u = [5 ± sqrt(25 - 16)] /8
sqrt(9) is 3, so:

u = (5 ±3)/8

So two solutions:

First solution: (5+3)/8 = 8/8=1
Second solution: (5-3)/8=2/8=1/4

Therefore, u can be 1 or 1/4.

Case 1: u=1 => m^2=1 => m=±1

Case 2: u=1/4 => m^2=1/4 => m=±1/2

The slopes of the two lines are either both \(1\) and \(2\), \(-1\) and \(-2\), \(0.5\) and \(1\), or \(-0.5\) and \(-1\). 

Step-by-step explanation:

1. Let the slope of one line be \(m\).
2. The slope of the other line is then \(2m\).
3. The angle between two lines with slopes \(m_1\) and \(m_2\) is given by:
   \[
   \tan(\theta) = \left|\frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1m_2}\right|
   \]
4. Substitute \(m_1 = m\) and \(m_2 = 2m\):
   \[
   \tan(\theta) = \left|\frac{2m - m}{1 + m(2m)}\right| = \left|\frac{m}{1 + 2m^2}\right|
   \]
5. Given that \(\tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{3}\), set up the equation:
   \[
   \left|\frac{m}{1 + 2m^2}\right| = \frac{1}{3}
   \]
6. Remove the absolute value and solve for \(m\):
   \[
   m = \pm \frac{1 + 2m^2}{3}
   \]
7. Multiply both sides by 3:
   \[
   3m = 1 + 2m^2
   \]
8. Rearranging gives the quadratic equation:
   \[
   2m^2 - 3m + 1 = 0
   \]
9. Solve using the quadratic formula:
   \[
   m = \frac{3 \pm \sqrt{9 - 8}}{4} = \frac{3 \pm 1}{4}
   \]
   So, \(m = 1\) or \(m = 0.5\).
10. Therefore, the slopes are either \(1\) and \(2\) (since \(2m = 2\)) or \(0.5\) and \(1\) (since \(2m = 1\)).
11. Considering negative values, if \(m = -1\), then \(2m = -2\); similarly for \(m = -0.5\), \(2m = -1\).
12. Thus, the possible pairs are \((1, 2)\), \((-1, -2)\), \((0.5, 1)\), and \((-0.5, -1)\).

Final Answer:
The slopes of the two lines are either \(1\) and \(2\), or \(-1\) and \(-2\), or \(0.5\) and \(1\), or \(-0.5\) and \(-1\).

The equation of the straight line that is parallel to the x-axis and passes through the point (0, 5) can be found using the slope-intercept form \( y = mx + b \). Since it's parallel to the x-axis, its slope (\( m \)) is 0. Therefore, the equation simplifies to \( y = b \).

Given that the line passes through (0, 5), substituting these coordinates into the equation gives:

\[
5 = 0 \times 0 + b
\]
So, \( b = 5 \).

Thus, the equation of the straight line is:
\[ y = 5 \]

To determine if the three given points \((3, 0)\), \((-2, -2)\), and \((8, 2)\) are collinear, we can use the concept of slope. If the slopes between each pair of points are equal, then the points lie on a straight line.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Calculate the slope between \((3, 0)\) and \((-2, -2)\):**

   The formula for slope (\(m\)) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]

   Substituting the given values:
   \[
   m_{1} = \frac{-2 - 0}{-2 - 3} = \frac{-2}{-5} = \frac{2}{5}
   \]

2. **Calculate the slope between \((-2, -2)\) and \((8, 2)\):**

   Using the same slope formula:
   \[
   m_{2} = \frac{2 - (-2)}{8 - (-2)} = \frac{4}{10} = \frac{2}{5}
   \]

3. **Compare the slopes:**

   Both slopes \(m_{1}\) and \(m_{2}\) are equal (\(\frac{2}{5}\)). Therefore, all three points lie on a straight line.

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{\text{Yes}}
\]

Let's find the equation of the median drawn from vertex \( P(2, 1) \) in triangle \( \Delta PQR \).

**Step 1: Identify the Midpoint of Side \( QR \)**

The coordinates of vertices are:
- \( Q(-2, 3) \)
- \( R(4, 5) \)

To find the midpoint (\( M \)) of side \( QR \):
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_Q + x_R}{2}, \frac{y_Q + y_R}{2} \right) = \left( \frac{-2 + 4}{2}, \frac{3 + 5}{2} \right) = (1, 4)
\]

**Step 2: Calculate the Slope of Side \( QR \)**

The slope (\( m_{QR} \)) of side \( QR \) is:
\[
m_{QR} = \frac{y_R - y_Q}{x_R - x_Q} = \frac{5 - 3}{4 - (-2)} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 3: Determine the Slope of the Median**

Since the median is perpendicular to \( QR \), its slope (\( m_{median} \)) is the negative reciprocal of \( m_{QR} \):
\[
m_{median} = -\frac{1}{m_{QR}} = -3
\]

**Step 4: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation**

Using point-slope form with point \( P(2, 1) \) and slope \( -3 \):
\[
y - y_P = m_{median}(x - x_P)
\]
\[
y - 1 = -3(x - 2)
\]
Simplifying:
\[
y - 1 = -3x + 6
\]
\[
y = -3x + 7
\]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the median from vertex \( P \) is:

\[
\boxed{y\ =\ -3x\ +\ 7}

**Solution:**

We are given two points: \( (H, 5) \) and \( (6, 3) \), and the line equation \( 7x - 9y - 19 = 0 \). We need to determine the value of \( H \) such that these two lines intersect at a right angle.

**Step 1: Verify if \( (H, 5) \) lies on \( 7x - 9y - 19 = 0 \)**

Substitute \( y = 5 \) into the equation:
\[
7H - 45 - 19 = 0 \\
7H - 64 = 0 \\
7H = 64 \\
H = \frac{64}{7}
\]

**Step 2: Calculate the slope of the line joining \( (H, 5) \) and \( (6, 3) \)**

The slope (\( m_1 \)) is:
\[
m_1 = \frac{3 - 5}{6 - H} = \frac{-2}{6 - H}
\]

**Step 3: Determine the slope of the given line \( 7x - 9y - 19 = 0 \)**

Rearrange to slope-intercept form:
\[
9y = 7x - 19 \\
y = \frac{7}{9}x - \frac{19}{9}
\]
So, the slope (\( m_2 \)) is \( \frac{7}{9} \).

**Step 4: Ensure perpendicularity of the two lines**

For two lines to be perpendicular:
\[
m_1 \times m_2 = -1
\]

Substitute the slopes:
\[
\left( \frac{-2}{6 - H} \right) \times \left( \frac{7}{9} \right) = -1 \\
\frac{-14}{54 - 9H} = -1 \\
\frac{14}{54 - 9H} = 1 \\
14 = 54 - 9H \\
9H = 54 - 14 \\
9H = 40 \\
H = \frac{40}{9}
\]

**Final Answer:**

The value of \( H \) that satisfies the conditions is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{40}{9}}

To determine the angle between the two lines given by the equations:

\[
\sqrt{3}x + y = 1
\]
and 
\[
x + \sqrt{3}y = 1
\]

**Step 1: Find the Slopes of Both Lines**

First, we'll rewrite each equation in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)) to identify their slopes.

1. For the first line:
   \[
   \sqrt{3}x + y = 1 \\
   y = -\sqrt{3}x + 1
   \]
   So, the slope \(m_1\) is \(-\sqrt{3}\).

2. For the second line:
   \[
   x + \sqrt{3}y = 1 \\
   \sqrt{3}y = -x + 1 \\
   y = \left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)x + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
   \]
   So, the slope \(m_2\) is \(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\).

**Step 2: Use the Formula to Find the Angle Between Two Lines**

The formula to find the angle θ between two lines with slopes \(m_1\) and \(m_2\) is:

\[
\tan\theta = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]

Substitute the values of \(m_1\) and \(m_2\):

\[
\tan\theta = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-\sqrt{3})}{1 + \left(-\sqrt{3}\right) \cdot \left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)} \right|
\]

**Step 3: Simplify the Expression**

Simplify the numerator and denominator:

- **Numerator:**
  \[
  -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} + \sqrt{3} = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{1} = \frac{-1 + 3}{\sqrt{3}} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
  \]

- **Denominator:**
  \[
  1 + (\sqrt{3} \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}) = 1 + 1 = 2
  \]

Now, compute \(\tan\theta\):

\[
\tan\theta = \left| \frac{\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right| = \left| \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}} \times \frac{1}{2} \right| = \left| \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \right|
\]

So, 

\[
\tan\theta = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
\]

**Step 4: Find the Angle θ**

We know that:

\[
\theta = \arctan\left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \right) = \frac{\pi}{6} \text{ radians}
\]

**Final Answer**

The angle between the two lines is:

```json
{"Final Answer": "π/6"}
```

   The general form of a straight line using intercepts is:
   \[
   \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
   \]
   where \( a \) is the x-intercept and \( b \) is the y-intercept.

3. **Substitute the Known X-Intercept:**
   
   Given that the x-intercept \( a = 5 \), we substitute into the equation:
   \[
   \frac{x}{5} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
   \]

Lines are parallel if they have the same slope. To determine the slope of the given line, I'll rearrange its equation into the slope-intercept form \( y = mx + b \).

\[ 5x - 6y + 4 = 0 \]

Subtracting \( 5x \) and adding 4 to both sides gives:
\[ -6y = -5x - 4 \]

\[ y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3} \]

From this, I can see that the slope \( m \) is \( \frac{5}{6} \).

Next, using the point-slope form of a line's equation with the known slope and the given point (-4, 5), I set up:
\[ y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4) \]

To convert this into standard form \( Ax + By + C = 0 \), I'll eliminate the fraction by multiplying both sides by 6:
\[ 6(y - 5) = 5(x + 4) \]

To find the equation of a line parallel to \( 5x - 6y + 4 = 0 \) that passes through the point (-4, 5), follow these steps:

1. **Determine the Slope of the Given Line:**
   
   The given line is:
   \[
   5x - 6y + 4 = 0
   \]
   
   To find its slope, rearrange it into the slope-intercept form \( y = mx + b \):
   \[
   -6y = -5x - 4 \\
   y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
   \]
   
   The slope (\( m \)) of the given line is \( \frac{5}{6} \).

2. **Use the Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Parallel Line:**
   
   Since parallel lines have the same slope, the new line will also have a slope of \( \frac{5}{6} \).
   
   Using the point-slope form:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Substitute the point (-4, 5) and \( m = \frac{5}{6} \):
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
   \]

3. **Convert to Standard Form:**
   
   Multiply both sides by 6 to eliminate the fraction:
   \[
   6(y - 5) = 5(x + 4) \\
   6y - 30 = 5x + 20
   \]
   
   Rearrange to get all terms on one side:
   \[
   5x - 6y - 50 = 0
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{5x - 6y - 50 = 0}
\]

First, I need to identify which two parallel lines are given by the equations \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\) and \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\). 

To check if they are parallel, I'll compare their coefficients. The first equation can be rewritten as \(38x + 24y - 76 = 0\) by multiplying both sides by 2. This shows that the second equation is a multiple of the first one, confirming that they are indeed parallel.

Next, to find the distance between these two lines, I'll use the formula for the distance \(d\) between two parallel lines \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\), which is:

\[ d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

Substituting the values from our equations, where \(C_1 = -76\) and \(C_2 = 70\):

\[ d = \frac{|-76 - 70|}{\sqrt{38^2 + 24^2}} = \frac{146}{\sqrt{1444 + 576}} = \frac{146}{\sqrt{2020}} \]

Simplifying the square root:

\[ \sqrt{2020} = 2\sqrt{505} \]

So, the distance becomes:

\[ d = \frac{146}{2\sqrt{505}} = \frac{73}{\sqrt{505}} \]

Finally, rationalizing the denominator gives:

\[ d = \frac{73\sqrt{505}}{505} \]

This is the distance between the two given parallel lines.
</think>

To find the distance between the two parallel straight lines \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\) and \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Lines
First, observe that both equations are linear. To determine if they are parallel:
- Multiply the first equation by 2 to align coefficients:
  
  \[
  2 \times (19x + 12y - 38) = 0 \\
  38x + 24y - 76 = 0
  \]
  
  Now, compare with the second equation \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\).

### Step 2: Confirm Parallelism
Since both equations have identical coefficients for \(x\) and \(y\), they are **parallel**.

### Step 3: Use Distance Formula Between Two Parallel Lines
The formula to calculate the distance (\(d\)) between two parallel lines \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\) is:

\[
d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

### Step 4: Apply the Values
From our equations:
- First line (\(L_1\)): \(38x + 24y - 76 = 0\) → \(C_1 = -76\)
- Second line (\(L_2\)): \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\) → \(C_2 = 70\)

Plugging these into the formula:

\[
d = \frac{|-76 - 70|}{\sqrt{38^2 + 24^2}} \\
d = \frac{146}{\sqrt{1444 + 576}} \\
d = \frac{146}{\sqrt{2020}}
\]

Simplifying the square root:

\[
\sqrt{2020} = 2\sqrt{505}
\]

Thus,

\[
d = \frac{146}{2\sqrt{505}} \\
d = \frac{73}{\sqrt{505}}
\]

To rationalize the denominator:

\[
d = \frac{73\sqrt{505}}{505}
\]

### Final Answer
The distance between the two parallel lines is:

\[
D = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

   - Point \( (x_1, y_1) = (1, 2) \)
   - Line equation: \( 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 \)
     - Here, \( A = 4 \), \( B = 7 \), and \( C = 5 \)

To find the equation of a straight line that is parallel to the lines \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\) and \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\), follow these steps:

1. **Determine the Slope:**

   Both given lines can be rewritten in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)) to identify their slopes.

   - For \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\):
     \[
     6y = -9x + 7 \\
     y = -\frac{3}{2}x + \frac{7}{6}
     \]
     **Slope (\(m\))**: \(-\frac{3}{2}\)

   - For \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\):
     \[
     2y = -3x - 6 \\
     y = -\frac{3}{2}x - 3
     \]
     **Slope (\(m\))**: \(-\frac{3}{2}\)

   Since both lines have the same slope, they are parallel.

2. **General Form of a Parallel Line:**

   Any line parallel to these will have the same slope but a different constant term in its equation. The general form is:
   \[
   9x + 6y + c = 0
   \]

\[ \text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2} \]

So, the distance from \( P(x, 0) \) to (9, 8) is:
\[ D_1 = \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2} \]
Simplifying:
\[ D_1 = \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64} \]

Similarly, the distance from \( P(x, 0) \) to (5, 6) is:
\[ D_2 = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2} \]

\[ D_2 = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36} \]

\[ \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36} \]

To eliminate the square roots, I'll square both sides:
\[ (9 - x)^2 + 64 = (5 - x)^2 + 36 \]

\[ 84 = 8x \]
Dividing both sides by 8 to solve for \( x \):
\[ x = 10.5 \]

Therefore, the point on the x-axis that is equidistant from (9, 8) and (5, 6) is (10.5, 0).

First, I'll find the midpoint M between points P(2, -2) and B(10, 2). The midpoint's coordinates are the average of the corresponding x-coordinates and y-coordinates. So, for the x-coordinate: (2 + 10)/2 = 6, and for the y-coordinate: (-2 + 2)/2 = 0. Therefore, M is at (6, 0).

Next, I'll calculate the slope of the line passing through P and B using the formula (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁). Substituting the values gives (2 - (-2))/(10 - 2) = 4/8 = 0.5.

With the slope known, the equation of the line in point-slope form is y - (-2) = 0.5(x - 2), which simplifies to y + 2 = 0.5x - 1. Rearranging terms yields y = 0.5x - 3.

To find the slope (ڈھلینگ) of the line passing through points \( P(2, -2) \) and \( B(10, 2) \), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Find the Midpoint**

The midpoint \( M \) between two points \( P(x_1, y_1) \) and \( B(x_2, y_2) \) is calculated using:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
M = \left( \frac{2 + 10}{2}, \frac{-2 + 2}{2} \right) = (6, 0)
\]

**Step 2: Calculate the Slope**

The slope \( m \) of the line passing through two points \( P(x_1, y_1) \) and \( B(x_2, y_2) \) is calculated using:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{2 - (-2)}{10 - 2} = \frac{4}{8} = 0.5
\]

**Step 3: Equation of the Line**

Using the point-slope form with point \( P(2, -2) \):
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Substituting:
\[
y - (-2) = 0.5(x - 2)
\]
Simplify:
\[
y + 2 = 0.5x - 1 \\
y = 0.5x - 3
\]

**Step 4: Verify Midpoint Lies on the Line**

Substitute \( M(6, 0) \) into the equation:
\[
0 = 0.5 \times 6 - 3 \\
0 = 3 - 3 \\
0 = 0 \quad \text{(True)}
\]
This confirms that midpoint \( M \) lies on line \( AB \).

**Final Answer:**

The slope of the line is **0.5**, and it passes through the midpoint \( (6, 0) \). The equation of the line is:
\[
y = 0.5x - 3
\]

If two lines are perpendicular, their slopes should multiply to -1. So maybe I can find the slopes of the lines connecting these points and see if any pair multiplies to -1.

First, let me label the points for clarity. Let's call them A(4,4), B(3,5), and C(-1,-1).

I'll start by finding the slope between two points at a time. The formula for slope between two points (x1,y1) and (x2,y2) is m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1). I need to calculate three slopes: AB, BC, and AC.

Calculating slope AB:
Points A(4,4) and B(3,5).
So, the change in y is 5-4=1.
Change in x is 3-4=-1.
Thus, slope m_AB = (1)/(-1) = -1.

Next, slope BC:
Points B(3,5) and C(-1,-1).
Change in y: -1 -5 = -6.
Change in x: -1 -3 = -4.
Slope m_BC = (-6)/(-4) = 6/4 = 3/2.

Now, slope AC:
Points A(4,4) and C(-1,-1).
Change in y: -1-4=-5.
Change in x: -1-4=-5.
So, slope m_AC = (-5)/(-5)=1.

Okay, so the three slopes are m_AB=-1, m_BC=3/2, and m_AC=1. Now, I need to check if any two of these slopes multiply to -1 because that would indicate perpendicular lines.

Checking AB and BC: (-1)*(3/2) = -3/2 ≠ -1.
Checking AB and AC: (-1)*1 = -1. Oh! That's exactly -1. So, the product is -1, which means these two sides are perpendicular to each other.

Therefore, since sides AB and AC have slopes that multiply to -1, they form a right angle at point A.

Wait, let me just double-check my calculations to make sure I didn't mix up any points.

Slope AB: (5-4)/(3-4)=1/-1=-1. Correct.
Slope BC: (-1-5)/(-1-3)=-6/-4=3/2. Correct.
Slope AC: (-1-4)/(-1-4)=-5/-5=1. Correct.

And the products:
AB & BC: -1*(3/2)= -3/2 ≠ -1
AB & AC: -1*1 = -1 ✔️

Yes, that's right. So point A is the vertex where the right angle is.

- The second side has a slope of -3, and its opposite side also has a slope of -3.

To determine whether the points \((-2, -1)\), \((4, 0)\), \((3, 3)\), and \((-3, 2)\) form a **parallelogram that is also a rectangle**, we can analyze their slopes without directly using the midpoint formula or distance formulas.

### Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Calculate Slopes of Adjacent Sides:**
   - **Slope between \((-2, -1)\) and \((4, 0)\):**
     \[
     m_1 = \frac{0 - (-1)}{4 - (-2)} = \frac{1}{6}
     \]
   
   - **Slope between \((4, 0)\) and \((3, 3)\):**
     \[
     m_2 = \frac{3 - 0}{3 - 4} = \frac{3}{-1} = -3
     \]
   
   - **Slope between \((3, 3)\) and \((-3, 2)\):**
     \[
     m_3 = \frac{2 - 3}{-3 - 3} = \frac{-1}{-6} = \frac{1}{6}
     \]
   
   - **Slope between \((-3, 2)\) and \((-2, -1)\):**
     \[
     m_4 = \frac{-1 - 2}{-2 - (-3)} = \frac{-3}{1} = -3
     \]

2. **Determine Parallelism:**
   - Compare the slopes of opposite sides:
     - \(m_1 = \frac{1}{6}\) is parallel to \(m_3 = \frac{1}{6}\)
     - \(m_2 = -3\) is parallel to \(m_4 = -3\)

   Since both pairs of opposite sides have equal slopes, the points lie on two sets of parallel lines. This confirms that they form a **parallelogram**.

3. **Check for Rectangle:**
   - In a rectangle, all adjacent sides must be perpendicular (their dot product is zero).
   
   - Calculate the vectors representing the sides:
     - Vector 1 (\(v_1\)): from \((-2, -1)\) to \((4, 0)\) is \((6, 1)\)
     - Vector 2 (\(v_2\)): from \((4, 0)\) to \((3, 3)\) is \((-1, 3)\)

   - Compute the dot product:
     \[
     v_1 \cdot v_2 = (6)(-1) + (1)(3) = -6 + 3 = -3
     \]
   
   Since the dot product is **not zero**, vectors \(v_1\) and \(v_2\) are not perpendicular. Therefore, the parallelogram is **not a rectangle**.

### Final Answer:

The problem involves finding the slopes of two straight lines where one slope is double the other, and the tangent of the angle between them is \( \frac{1}{3} \). 

Let’s denote the slopes as \( m \) and \( 2m \). Using the formula for the tangent of the angle between two lines:

\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]

Substituting \( m_1 = m \) and \( m_2 = 2m \):

\[
\frac{1}{3} = \left| \frac{2m - m}{1 + m(2m)} \right| \\
\Rightarrow \frac{1}{3} = \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right|
\]

Solving for \( m \):

\[
3m = 1 + 2m^2 \\
2m^2 - 3m + 1 = 0
\]

Using the quadratic formula:

\[
m = \frac{3 \pm \sqrt{9 - 8}}{4} \\
m = \frac{3 \pm 1}{4}
\]

So, \( m = 1 \) or \( m = 0.5 \). Therefore, the slopes are \( 0.5 \) and \( 1 \).

To determine if the points \((3, 0)\), \((-2, -2)\), and \((8, 2)\) are **collinear** (lying on the same straight line), we can use the concept of slopes. If the slope between any two pairs of points is the same, then all three points lie on the same straight line.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Calculate the Slope Between the First Two Points:**
   - Points: \((3, 0)\) and \((-2, -2)\)
   - **Slope Formula:** 
     \[
     m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
     \]
   - Plugging in the values:
     \[
     m_{1} = \frac{-2 - 0}{-2 - 3} = \frac{-2}{-5} = \frac{2}{5}
     \]

2. **Calculate the Slope Between the Second and Third Points:**
   - Points: \((-2, -2)\) and \((8, 2)\)
   - Using the same slope formula:
     \[
     m_{2} = \frac{2 - (-2)}{8 - (-2)} = \frac{4}{10} = \frac{2}{5}
     \]

3. **Compare the Slopes:**
   - Since \(m_{1} = m_{2} = \frac{2}{5}\), all three points lie on a straight line.

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{\text{Yes}}

The original line passes through (0, 2) with a slope of −√3, so its equation is y = −√3x + 2. To find lines parallel to this at a distance of 2 units away:

1. Rewrite the original equation in standard form: √3x + y = 2.
2. The general form for a parallel line is √3x + y = C, where |C - 2| / √( (√3)^2 + 1^2 ) = 2 ⇒ |C − 2| = 4.
3. Solving gives C = 6 and C = -2.

Thus, the parallel lines are:
- √3x + y = 6 → y = −√3x + 6 (intercept at (0, 6))
- √3x + y = -2 → y = −√3x − 2 (intercept at (0, −2))

**Answer:**
The equations of the two parallel lines are \boxed{y = -\sqrt{3}x + 6} and \boxed{y = -\sqrt{3}x - 2}.

Given the problem, we need to find the equation of a hyperbola with vertices at \( (-5, 0) \) and \( (5, 0) \) such that for any point on the hyperbola, the sum of distances from two fixed points is constant. 

1. **Identify the type of conic section**: The given problem involves a hyperbola because it refers to the difference of distances being constant.
2. **Determine the standard form of the hyperbola**: Since the vertices are on the x-axis, the hyperbola opens left and right, and its equation is of the form \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \).
3. **Calculate \( a \) and \( b \)**: Given vertices at \( (-5, 0) \) and \( (5, 0) \), we find \( a = 5 \). The foci are given as \( (c, 0) \) where \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \).
4. **Formulate the equation**: Using the relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \), we derive the hyperbola's equation.

After detailed calculations, we find that the equation of the hyperbola is:

\[
xy = 32 - 4\sqrt{233}
\]

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{xy = 32 - 4\sqrt{233}}
\]

To determine the equation of a line with equal magnitude intercepts on both axes that passes through (4,5), we consider two cases based on the condition |a|=|b|, where 'a' and 'b' are the x-intercept and y-intercept respectively.

Case 1: Both intercepts have the same sign.
- The equation is \( \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{a} = 1 \).
- Substituting (4,5), we get \( a=9 \). Thus, the equation is \( x + y = 9 \).

Case 2: Intercepts have opposite signs.
- The equation becomes \( \frac{x}{a} - \frac{y}{a} = 1 \).
- Substituting (4,5), we find \( a=-1 \). Thus, the equation is \( x - y = -1 \) or equivalently \( y = x + 1 \).

Therefore, there are two possible lines:  
\( x + y = 9 \) and \( y = x + 1 \).

However, if considering only positive intercepts, then \( x + y =9 \) would be the answer.

Answer: \( x + y = 9 \).

The equation of the straight line passing through points (1, 0) and (2, 3) is found by calculating the slope and using one of the points to determine the y-intercept. The slope \( m \) is calculated as:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{3 - 0}{2 - 1} = 3
\]

Using point (1, 0), the equation becomes:

\[
0 = 3(1) + b \implies b = -3
\]

Thus, the equation of the line is \( y = 3x - 3 \).

**Answer:**  
The equation of the straight line is \boxed{y = 3x - 3}.

To find the equation of a line perpendicular to the one passing through points (4,7) and (-5,8), follow these steps:

1. **Find the slope of the original line:**
   The slope \( m \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = -\frac{1}{9}
   \]
   
2. **Determine the perpendicular slope:**
   The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal, so:
   \[
   m_{\text{perpendicular}} = 9
   \]

3. **Use point-slope form with one of the points:**
   - Using (4,7):
     \[
     y - 7 = 9(x - 4) \\
     y = 9x - 36 + 7 \\
     y = 9x - 29
     \]
   
   - Alternatively, using (-5,8):
     \[
     y - 8 = 9(x + 5) \\
     y = 9x + 45 + 8 \\
     y = 9x + 53
     \]

**Final Answer:**
The equations of the lines perpendicular to the one through (4,7) and (-5,8) are:
\[
y = 9x - 29 \quad \text{and} \quad y = 9x + 53
\]

Alright, so I've got this problem where I need to find the equation of the median from vertex A (which is at point P) in triangle PQR. The coordinates given are P(2,1), Q(-2,3), and R(4,5). 

First off, let me make sure I understand what a median is. In a triangle, a median is a line segment joining a vertex to the midpoint of the opposite side. So in this case, from point A (P) to the midpoint of side QR.

Okay, so step one is to find the midpoint of side QR. The coordinates for Q are (-2,3) and for R are (4,5). To find the midpoint M, I can use the midpoint formula: 

M = ((x_Q + x_R)/2, (y_Q + y_R)/2)

Plugging in the values:

x-coordinate of M: (-2 + 4)/2 = 2/2 = 1
y-coordinate of M: (3 + 5)/2 = 8/2 = 4

So midpoint M is at (1,4).

Now I need to find the equation of the line that passes through point P(2,1) and this midpoint M(1,4). To do this, first, I should calculate the slope (m) of the line PM.

Slope formula: m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1)

Using points P(2,1) as (x1,y1) and M(1,4) as (x2,y2):

m = (4 - 1)/(1 - 2) = 3/(-1) = -3

So the slope is -3.

With the slope known, I can use the point-slope form of a line to find the equation. The formula is:

y - y1 = m(x - x1)

Using point P(2,1):

y - 1 = -3(x - 2)

Simplifying this:

y - 1 = -3x + 6
y = -3x + 6 + 1
y = -3x + 7

So the equation of the median is y = -3x + 7.

Wait, but I should probably write it in standard form as well. To convert to standard form (Ax + By + C = 0), rearrange:

3x + y - 7 = 0

Or sometimes written as 3x + y = 7.

Let me double-check my calculations to make sure I didn't make any mistakes. 

Midpoint calculation: (-2+4)=2, divided by 2 is 1; (3+5)=8, divided by 2 is 4. Correct.

Slope between P(2,1) and M(1,4): (4-1)/(1-2)=3/-1=-3. Correct.

Equation using point-slope: y -1 = -3(x-2). Expanding gives y = -3x +6 +1 → y = -3x +7. Yes, that's correct.

So the final equation is 3x + y = 7.

The median from vertex A(2,1) to side QR with midpoint (1,4) has a slope of -3 and follows the equation:

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Coordinates:**
   - P(A): (2, 1)
   - Q: (-2, 3)
   - R: (4, 5)

2. **Find Midpoint M of QR:**
   - X-coordinate: (–2 + 4)/2 = 1
   - Y-coordinate: (3 + 5)/2 = 4
   - Midpoint M: (1, 4)

3. **Calculate Slope (m) Between P and M:**
   - m = (4 – 1)/(1 – 2) = 3/(-1) = -3

4. **Use Point-Slope Form to Find Equation:**
   - y – 1 = -3(x – 2)
   - Simplify:
     - y = -3x + 6 + 1
     - y = -3x + 7

5. **Express in Standard Form (if needed):**
   - 3x + y = 7

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the median is 3x + y = 7.

First, I need to determine the type of triangle formed by points A(4,5), B(6,1), and C(3,4).

**Solution:**

Given the vertices of triangle ABC:
- \( A(4,\ 5) \)
- \( B(6,\ 1) \)
- \( C(3,\ 4) \)

### Step 1: Classify the Triangle
First, we calculate the lengths of all sides to determine if the triangle is scalene or isosceles.

Using the distance formula:
\[
d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]

**Side AB:**
\[
AB = \sqrt{(6-4)^2 + (1-5)^2} = \sqrt{4 + 16} = \sqrt{20} ≈ 4.47
\]

**Side BC:**
\[
BC = \sqrt{(3-6)^2 + (4-1)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 9} = \sqrt{18} ≈ 4.24
\]

**Side AC:**
\[
AC = \sqrt{(3-4)^2 + (4-5)^2} = \sqrt{1 + 1} = \sqrt{2} ≈ 1.41
\]

Since all sides have different lengths, **triangle ABC is a scalene triangle**.

### Step 2: Find the Altitude Corresponding to Vertex A

To find the altitude from vertex \( A \) to side \( BC \):

1. **Find the equation of line BC**

   Coordinates of B and C:
   - \( B(6,\ 1) \)
   - \( C(3,\ 4) \)

   Slope of BC:
   \[
   m_{BC} = \frac{4 - 1}{3 - 6} = \frac{3}{-3} = -1
   \]

   Equation of line BC using point-slope form (using point B):
   \[
   y - 1 = -1(x - 6) \\
   y = -x + 7
   \]

2. **Calculate the perpendicular distance from A(4,5) to line BC**

   The formula for the distance \( d \) from a point \( (x_0, y_0) \) to the line \( ax + by + c = 0 \):
   \[
   d = \frac{|ax_0 + by_0 + c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
   \]

   Rewriting BC's equation:
   \[
   x + y - 7 = 0
   \]
   So, \( a = 1 \), \( b = 1 \), and \( c = -7 \).

   Substitute point A(4,5):
   \[
   d = \frac{|1\cdot4 + 1\cdot5 - 7|}{\sqrt{1^2 + 1^2}} \\
   d = \frac{|9 - 7|}{\sqrt{2}} \\
   d = \frac{2}{\sqrt{2}} = \sqrt{2} ≈ 1.41
   \]

### Final Answer:
The triangle ABC is a **scalene triangle**, and the length of the altitude from vertex A to side BC is \( \sqrt{2} \).

\]

\]

\]

\]

To solve for \( H \) such that both points \( (H, 5) \) and \( (6, 3) \) lie on the line defined by the equation \( 7x - 9y - 19 = 0 \), we first check if these points satisfy the given equation.

For point \( (H, 5) \):
\[
7H - 9(5) - 19 = 0 \\
7H - 45 - 19 = 0 \\
7H - 64 = 0 \\
7H = 64 \\
H = \frac{64}{7}
\]

For point \( (6, 3) \):
\[
7(6) - 9(3) - 19 = 42 - 27 - 19 = -4 \neq 0
\]
Thus, \( (6, 3) \) does not lie on the line.

Since both points must lie on the given line for them to be connected by it, and only \( H = \frac{64}{7} \) satisfies this condition, we conclude that:

**Answer:** 
\( H = \boxed{\dfrac{64}{7}} \)

First, I'll identify the two lines given by their equations:

1. \( \sqrt{3}x + y = 1 \)
2. \( x + \sqrt{3}y = 1 \)

To find the angle between these lines, I need to determine their slopes.

For the first line:
\( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 1 \)
So, the slope (\( m_1 \)) is \( -\sqrt{3} \).

For the second line:
\( \sqrt{3}y = -x + 1 \)  
\( y = (-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}})x + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \)
So, the slope (\( m_2 \)) is \( -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \).

Next, I'll use the formula for the angle between two lines:
\( \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1m_2} \right| \)

Plugging in the slopes:
\( \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-\sqrt{3})}{1 + (-\sqrt{3})(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}})} \right| \)
Simplifying the numerator and denominator:
Numerator: \( \sqrt{3} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} = \frac{2\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{3}} = 2 \)  
Denominator: \( 1 + 1 = 2 \)

So, \( \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{2}{2} \right| = 1 \), which means \( \theta = 45^\circ \).
</think>

To find the angle between the two lines given by the equations:

\[
\sqrt{3}x + y = 1
\]
and 
\[
x + \sqrt{3}y = 1
\]

follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Slopes of the Lines

First, convert both equations into slope-intercept form \( y = mx + b \), where \( m \) is the slope.

**For the first line:**
\[
\sqrt{3}x + y = 1
\]
Subtract \( \sqrt{3}x \) from both sides:
\[
y = -\sqrt{3}x + 1
\]
So, the slope (\( m_1 \)) is:
\[
m_1 = -\sqrt{3}
\]

**For the second line:**
\[
x + \sqrt{3}y = 1
\]
Subtract \( x \) from both sides and divide by \( \sqrt{3} \):
\[
\sqrt{3}y = -x + 1 \\
y = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}x + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
\]
So, the slope (\( m_2 \)) is:
\[
m_2 = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
\]

### Step 2: Use the Formula for the Angle Between Two Lines

The formula to find the angle \( \theta \) between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) is:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]

Plugging in the values:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-\sqrt{3})}{1 + (-\sqrt{3})(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}})} \right|
\]
Simplify the numerator and denominator:
\[
= \left| \frac{\sqrt{3} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}}{1 + 1} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{\frac{2\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{2}{2} \right| = 1
\]

So,
\[
\tan(\theta) = 1 \implies \theta = 45^\circ
\]

### Final Answer

The angle between the two lines is:

```json
{"Final Answer": "45°"}
```

To find the equation of a line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through the point \((-4, 5)\), follow these steps:

1. **Find the Slope of the Given Line:**
   
   Start by rearranging the given equation into slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):
   \[
   5x - 6y + 4 = 0 \\
   -6y = -5x - 4 \\
   y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
   \]
   
   The slope (\(m\)) of the given line is \(\frac{5}{6}\).

2. **Determine the Slope of the Parallel Line:**
   
   Since parallel lines have equal slopes, the slope of the new line will also be \(\frac{5}{6}\).

3. **Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the New Line:**
   
   Using the point-slope form (\(y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)\)) with the point \((-4, 5)\):
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
   \]
   
4. **Convert to Standard Form (\(Ax + By + C = 0\)):**
   
   Multiply both sides by 6 to eliminate the fraction:
   \[
   6(y - 5) = 5(x + 4) \\
   6y - 30 = 5x + 20
   \]
   
   Rearranging terms gives:
   \[
   -5x + 6y - 50 = 0
   \]
   
   To match the standard form, we can write it as:
   \[
   5x - 6y + 50 = 0
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{5x - 6y + 50 = 0}
\]

To determine the distance between two parallel lines, I first need to confirm that both equations represent parallel lines. This can be done by comparing their slopes.

The given equations are:
19x + 12y - 38 = 0  
and  
38x + 24y + 70 = 0

Calculating the slope of each line:
For the first equation, solving for y gives me a slope of -19/12.
For the second equation, simplifying it shows that it also has a slope of -19/12.

Since both slopes are equal, the lines are indeed parallel. This means they will never intersect and their distance apart is constant throughout.

Next, I'll use the formula for the distance between two parallel lines:
Distance = |c1 - c2| / sqrt(a^2 + b^2)

Rewriting both equations in the standard form ax + by + c = 0:

First equation:  
19x + 12y - 38 = 0

Second equation:  
38x + 24y + 70 = 0

To make sure a and b are consistent between both equations, I'll divide the second equation by 2:
19x + 12y + 35 = 0

Now, c1 is -38 (from the first equation) and c2 is 35 (from the second equation).

Calculating |c1 - c2|:  
|-38 - 35| = |-73| = 73

Calculating sqrt(a^2 + b^2):  
sqrt(19^2 + 12^2) = sqrt(361 + 144) = sqrt(505)

Therefore, the distance between the two parallel lines is:
73 / sqrt(505)
</think>

To find the distance between the two **parallel** straight lines given by the equations:

\[
19x + 12y - 38 = 0
\]
and 
\[
38x + 24y + 70 = 0
\]

follow these steps:

### Step 1: Confirm Parallelism
First, ensure that both lines are parallel by checking their slopes.

- **Line 1:** \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\)
  
  Solving for \(y\) gives:
  \[
  y = -\frac{19}{12}x + \frac{38}{12}
  \]
  
  So, the slope (\(m_1\)) is \(-\frac{19}{12}\).

- **Line 2:** \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\)
  
  Solving for \(y\) gives:
  \[
  y = -\frac{38}{24}x - \frac{70}{24}
  \]
  
  Simplify the slope (\(m_2\)) to \(-\frac{19}{12}\).

Since both slopes are equal (\(m_1 = m_2\)), the lines are indeed **parallel**.

### Step 2: Use Distance Formula for Parallel Lines
The formula to find the distance between two parallel lines \(ax + by + c_1 = 0\) and \(ax + by + c_2 = 0\) is:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|c_1 - c_2|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

### Step 3: Rewrite the Equations in Standard Form
Ensure both equations are in the form \(ax + by + c = 0\).

- **Line 1:** \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\)  
  Here, \(a = 19\), \(b = 12\), and \(c_1 = -38\).

- **Line 2:** To match the coefficients of Line 1:
  
  Divide the entire equation by 2 to simplify:
  \[
  19x + 12y + 35 = 0
  \]
  
  Here, \(a = 19\), \(b = 12\), and \(c_2 = 35\).

### Step 4: Apply the Distance Formula
Now, plug in the values into the distance formula:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|-38 - 35|}{\sqrt{19^2 + 12^2}} = \frac{73}{\sqrt{361 + 144}} = \frac{73}{\sqrt{505}}
\]

### Final Answer
The distance between the two parallel lines is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{73}{\sqrt{505}}}
\]

   \]

To find a point on the **x-axis** that is equidistant from the points \((9, 8)\) and \((5, 6)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Problem
We need to locate a point \( P(x, 0) \) on the x-axis such that its distance from both given points is equal.

### Step 2: Use the Distance Formula
The distance between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
\[
\sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]

Set up the distances from \( P(x, 0) \) to both points:

- Distance to \((9, 8)\):
  \[
  \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64}
  \]

- Distance to \((5, 6)\):
  \[
  \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36}
  \]

### Step 3: Set Distances Equal and Solve for \(x\)
Since the distances are equal:
\[
\sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36}
\]

Square both sides to eliminate the square roots:
\[
(9 - x)^2 + 64 = (5 - x)^2 + 36
\]

Expand and simplify:
\[
81 - 18x + x^2 + 64 = 25 - 10x + x^2 + 36 \\
145 - 18x = 61 - 10x \\

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Substituting the given coordinates into the formula:

\[
m = \frac{2 - (-2)}{10 - 2} = \frac{4}{8} = 0.5
\]

Next, to find the midpoint (\( M \)) between \( P(2, -2) \) and \( B(10, 2) \), we use the midpoint formula:

\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]

Calculating the coordinates of \( M \):

\[
M_x = \frac{2 + 10}{2} = 6
\]
\[
M_y = \frac{-2 + 2}{2} = 0
\]

Therefore, the midpoint is at \( (6, 0) \).

**Final Answer:**

The slope of the line segment connecting points \( P(2, -2) \) and \( B(10, 2) \) is **0.5**, and the midpoint between these two points is **\( (6, 0) \)**.

\text{Slope} = 0.5, \quad \text{Midpoint} = (6, 0)

The problem involves finding the slope of a straight line that makes a 60° angle with the positive direction of the y-axis. To determine this, we first convert the given angle relative to the y-axis into an angle relative to the x-axis.

1. **Convert Angle Relative to Y-Axis**:  
   Since the angle is measured from the positive y-axis towards the positive x-axis in a counter-clockwise manner, it forms a 60° angle with the y-axis. This means the angle made with the positive x-axis (phi) is 90° minus 60°, which equals 30°.

2. **Calculate the Slope**:  
   The slope (m) of a line is given by the tangent of the angle it makes with the positive x-axis:
   \[
   m = \tan(\phi) = \tan(30^\circ)
   \]
   We know that \(\tan(30^\circ)\) equals \(1/\sqrt{3}\), which can also be expressed as \(\sqrt{3}/3\).

To determine if the given four points \((-2, -1)\), \((4, 0)\), \((3, 3)\), and \((-3, 2)\) form an **orthocentric system** (where at least one pair of lines is perpendicular), we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Calculate the Slopes Between Consecutive Points

We'll calculate the slopes between each pair of consecutive points to check for perpendicularity.

#### Slope Formula:
\[
\text{Slope} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

1. **Between \((-2, -1)\) and \((4, 0)\):**
   \[
   m_{AB} = \frac{0 - (-1)}{4 - (-2)} = \frac{1}{6}
   \]

2. **Between \((4, 0)\) and \((3, 3)\):**
   \[
   m_{BC} = \frac{3 - 0}{3 - 4} = -3
   \]

3. **Between \((3, 3)\) and \((-3, 2)\):**
   \[
   m_{CD} = \frac{2 - 3}{-3 - 3} = \frac{-1}{-6} = \frac{1}{6}
   \]

4. **Between \((-3, 2)\) and \((-2, -1)\):**
   \[
   m_{DA} = \frac{-1 - 2}{-2 - (-3)} = \frac{-3}{1} = -3
   \]

### Step 2: Check for Perpendicularity

Two lines are perpendicular if the product of their slopes is \(-1\).

Let's check each pair:

1. **\(m_{AB} \times m_{BC}\):**
   \[
   \frac{1}{6} \times (-3) = -\frac{1}{2} \neq -1
   \]

2. **\(m_{BC} \times m_{CD}\):**
   \[
   -3 \times \frac{1}{6} = -\frac{1}{2} \neq -1
   \]

3. **\(m_{CD} \times m_{DA}\):**
   \[
   \frac{1}{6} \times (-3) = -\frac{1}{2} \neq -1
   \]

4. **\(m_{DA} \times m_{AB}\):**
   \[
   -3 \times \frac{1}{6} = -\frac{1}{2} \neq -1
   \]

### Conclusion

None of the consecutive sides have slopes that are negative reciprocals, which would indicate perpendicularity. Therefore, **none** of these lines are perpendicular.

\boxed{\text{None}}

\]

The second line passes through the points (4, -2) and (6, 0). I'll calculate its slope as well.

The slope \( m \) for this line is:
\[

\]
With a slope of 1 and passing through (6, 0), the equation in point-slope form becomes:
\[
y - 0 = 1(x - 6) \\
y = x - 6
\]

Now that I have both equations:
- First line: \( y = x + 5 \)
- Second line: \( y = x - 6 \)

To find the angle between these two lines, I'll use the formula involving their slopes:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]
Substituting \( m_1 = 1 \) and \( m_2 = 1 \):
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{1 - 1}{1 + (1)(1)} \right| = \left| \frac{0}{2} \right| = 0
\]
This means:
\[
\theta = \arctan(0) = 0^\circ
\]

Therefore, the angle between the two lines is \( 0^\circ \), indicating that they are parallel.

The equation of the line is derived using the given slope and x-intercept.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Determine the Slope (m):**
   The tangent of the angle θ made by the line with the positive x-axis is given as 1/6. Therefore, the slope m = tanθ = 1/6.

2. **Use Point-Slope Form:**
   The x-intercept is at point (6, 0). Using the point-slope form of a line:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   Substituting \( m = \frac{1}{6} \), \( x_1 = 6 \), and \( y_1 = 0 \):
   \[
   y - 0 = \frac{1}{6}(x - 6)
   \]

3. **Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form:**
   Simplify the equation:
   \[
   y = \frac{1}{6}x - 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the line is \( y = \frac{1}{6}x - 1 \).

The equation of the straight line making an angle of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) radians (or 120 degrees) with the x-axis and passing through the point (0, 2) is found using the slope formula. The slope \( m = \tan(\frac{2\pi}{3}) = -\sqrt{3} \). Using the point-slope form of a line equation:

\[ y - 2 = -\sqrt{3}(x - 0) \]
Simplifying, we get:
\[ y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2 \]

For lines parallel to this one, they will have the same slope \( m = -\sqrt{3} \). Therefore, their equations are of the form:
\[ y = -\sqrt{3}x + c \]
where \( c \) is any constant.

For a line intersecting the y-axis at a specific distance from the origin along the y-axis, we can adjust the equation accordingly. Since the original line intersects the y-axis at (0, 2), if we need another line with slope -√3 but passing through a different point on the y-axis, say \( (0, c) \), its equation would be:
\[ y = -\sqrt{3}x + c \]

If the distance from the origin is required to be two units along the y-axis, we set \( |c| = 2 \). Thus, possible values of \( c \) are 2 and -2. However, since our original line has a positive intercept at (0, 2), another line with negative intercept could be considered depending on context.

Final Answer:
- The equation of the required line is: {Final Answer: "y = -√3x + 2"}
- Equations of parallel lines: y = -√3x + c where c is any constant
- Lines intersecting Y-axis at a distance of two units can be y = -√3x ± 2, depending on direction.

   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]

   \[

   \]

   7 = 0 + b \\
   b = 7
   \]

   \[

   y = 7

First, I need to find the distance from the point \((5, -7)\) to the line \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\). 

To do this, I'll use the formula for the distance from a point \((x_1, y_1)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\), which is:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Substituting the given values into the formula, I'll calculate the numerator and the denominator separately. Then, I'll divide the absolute value of the numerator by the square root of the sum of the squares of \(A\) and \(B\) to find the distance.
</think>

**Solution:**

To find the distance from the point \((5, -7)\) to the line \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\), we can use the formula for the distance from a point to a straight line:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

**Given:**
- The equation of the line is \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\).
- The coordinates of the point are \((5, -7)\).

**Substitute the values into the formula:**

1. **Calculate the numerator:**
   \[
   |A x_1 + B y_1 + C| = |5 \times 5 + (-6) \times (-7) - 28| = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39|
   \]

2. **Calculate the denominator:**
   \[
   \sqrt{A^2 + B^2} = \sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2} = \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61}
   \]

3. **Compute the distance:**
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}} \approx 4.97
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The distance from the point \((5, -7)\) to the line \(5x - 6y -28 = 0\) is approximately **4.97 units**.

√[(-2)^2 +1^2 ]=√5.

Thus triangle sides are 1,sqrt(2),sqrt(5),

so altitudes computed as above. Hence the results.

Alternatively, using area formula:

Area= |u x v| /2 =0.5,

then h_a= (2*0.5)/ sqrt(2)= 1/sqrt(2) =sqrt(2)/2,

h_b=(2*0.5)/1=1,

and h_c=(2*0.5)/sqrt(5)=1/sqrt(5)=sqrt(5)/5.

Yes, same results.

Therefore the altitudes are sqrt(2)/2, 1, and sqrt(5)/5.

**Final Answer**
The three heights (altitudes) of triangle \(OAB\) are \(\boxed{\dfrac{\sqrt{2}}{2}}\), \(\boxed{1}\), and \(\boxed{\dfrac{\sqrt{5}}{5}}\).
</think>

Given vectors \(\mathbf{u} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}\) and \(\mathbf{v} = \begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}\), we need to find the altitudes of triangle \(OAB\) where \(O\) is the origin, \(A\) is at \((1,0)\), and \(B\) is at \((-1,1)\).

First, we calculate the lengths of the sides of triangle \(OAB\):
- The length of \(OA\) is \(1\).
- The length of \(OB\) is \(\sqrt{(-1)^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{2}\).
- The length of \(AB\) is \(\sqrt{(1 - (-1))^2 + (0 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{4 + 1} = \sqrt{5}\).

Next, we find the area of triangle \(OAB\) using the magnitude of the cross product of vectors \(\mathbf{u}\) and \(\mathbf{v}\):
- The cross product is calculated as \(|1 \cdot 1 - 0 \cdot (-1)| = 1\).
- The area is \(\frac{1}{2} \times | \text{cross product} | = \frac{1}{2}\).

Using the area, we calculate the altitudes:
- The altitude from \(A\) (opposite side \(OB\)) is \(\frac{2 \times \text{Area}}{|OB|} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} = \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\).
- The altitude from \(B\) (opposite side \(OA\)) is \(\frac{2 \times \text{Area}}{|OA|} = \frac{1}{1} = 1\).
- The altitude from \(O\) (opposite side \(AB\)) is \(\frac{2 \times \text{Area}}{|AB|} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{5}} = \frac{\sqrt{5}}{5}\).

Thus, the three heights (altitudes) of triangle \(OAB\) are:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{\sqrt{2}}{2}}, \quad \boxed{1}, \quad \text{and} \quad \boxed{\dfrac{\sqrt{5}}{5}}
\]

First, I'll rewrite the given line in slope-intercept form to determine its slope. 

Starting with:
3x - 4y - 16 = 0

I'll solve for y:
-4y = -3x + 16  
Dividing both sides by -4:
y = (3/4)x - 4

So, the slope of the given line is 3/4.

Since perpendicular lines have slopes that are negative reciprocals, the slope of the perpendicular line will be -4/3.

Now, I'll use the point-slope form to write the equation of the perpendicular line passing through (1,5):
y - y₁ = m(x - x₁)

Plugging in the values:
y - 5 = (-4/3)(x - 1)

Multiplying both sides by 3 to eliminate the fraction:
3(y - 5) = -4(x - 1)
Expanding both sides:
3y - 15 = -4x + 4

Bringing all terms to one side:
4x + 3y - 19 = 0

So, the equation of the perpendicular line is 4x + 3y - 19 = 0.

To find the equation of a line that passes through the point (1, 5) and is perpendicular to the line \(3x - 4y - 16 = 0\), follow these steps:

1. **Determine the slope of the given line**:
   - Start with the equation: \(3x - 4y - 16 = 0\)
   - Rewrite it in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):
     \[
     -4y = -3x + 16 \\
     y = \frac{3}{4}x - 4
     \]
   - The slope (\(m\)) of the given line is \(\frac{3}{4}\).

2. **Find the slope of the perpendicular line**:
   - Perpendicular lines have slopes that are negative reciprocals.
   - The slope of the perpendicular line is \(-\frac{4}{3}\).

3. **Use the point-slope form to find the equation of the perpendicular line**:
   - Point-Slope Form: \(y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)\)
   - Plug in the point (1, 5) and slope \(-\frac{4}{3}\):
     \[
     y - 5 = -\frac{4}{3}(x - 1)
     \]
   
4. **Convert to standard form**:
   - Multiply both sides by 3 to eliminate the fraction:
     \[
     3(y - 5) = -4(x - 1)
     \]
   - Expand and simplify:
     \[
     3y - 15 = -4x + 4 \\
     4x + 3y - 19 = 0
     \]

**Final Answer**:  
The equation of the perpendicular line is \(4x + 3y - 19 = 0\).

To determine the value(s) of \( H \), we consider that the line passing through points \( (H,5) \) and \( (6,3) \) must have a slope of either 1 or -1 to ensure equal angles with both axes.

**Case 1:** Slope = 1  
\[
\frac{3 - 5}{6 - H} = 1 \\
\frac{-2}{6 - H} = 1 \\
-2 = 6 - H \\
H = 8
\]

**Case 2:** Slope = -1  
\[
\frac{3 - 5}{6 - H} = -1 \\
\frac{-2}{6 - H} = -1 \\
-2 = -(6 - H) \\
-2 = -6 + H \\
H = 4
\]

**Verification:**
Both \( H = 8 \) and \( H = 4 \) result in slopes of 1 and -1, respectively. These satisfy the condition that the line makes equal angles with both axes.

Thus, there are two possible solutions for \( H \): 8 and 4.

**Answer:** \( H = 8 \) or \( H = 4 \).

First, I need to find the angle between the two lines given by the equations √3x + y = 1 and x + √3y = 1.

To do this, I'll start by determining the slopes of each line. For the first equation, solving for y gives me y = -√3x + 1, so the slope (m₁) is -√3.

For the second equation, solving for y yields y = (-1/√3)x + 1/√3, making the slope (m₂) equal to -1/√3.

Next, I'll use the formula that relates the slopes of two lines with the angle between them: tan(θ) = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁m₂)|. Plugging in the values:

tan(θ) = |(-1/√3 - (-√3))/(1 + (-√3)(-1/√3))|.

Simplifying the numerator and denominator:

Numerator: (-1/√3 + √3) = (2√3)/√3 = 2

Denominator: 1 + 1 = 2

So, tan(θ) = |2 / 2| = 1.

Finally, taking the arctangent of both sides gives θ = 45 degrees.
</think>

To find the angle between the two lines given by the equations:

\[
\sqrt{3}x + y = 1 \quad \text{and} \quad x + \sqrt{3}y = 1
\]

**Step 1: Find the slopes of both lines**

For the first line, \( \sqrt{3}x + y = 1 \):
\[
y = -\sqrt{3}x + 1
\]
So, **Slope (m₁)** is:
\[
-\sqrt{3}
\]

For the second line, \( x + \sqrt{3}y = 1 \):
\[
\sqrt{3}y = -x + 1 \\
y = \frac{-1}{\sqrt{3}}x + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
\]
So, **Slope (m₂)** is:
\[
-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
\]

**Step 2: Use the formula to find the angle between two lines**

The formula to find the angle \( \theta \) between two lines with slopes \( m₁ \) and \( m₂ \) is:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m₂ - m₁}{1 + m₁m₂} \right|
\]

Plugging in the values:

\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-\sqrt{3})}{1 + \left(-\sqrt{3}\right)\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)} \right|
\]

Simplify the numerator and denominator:

**Numerator:**
\[
-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} + \sqrt{3} = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{1} \\
= \frac{-1 + 3}{\sqrt{3}} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
\]

**Denominator:**
\[
1 + (\sqrt{3} \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}) = 1 + 1 = 2
\]

So,
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right| = \left| \frac{2}{2\sqrt{3}} \right| = \left| \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \right|
\]

**Step 3: Find the angle \( \theta \)**

Since,
\[
\tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
\]
We know that:
\[
\theta = \arctan\left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \right) = 30^\circ
\]

**Final Answer:**

The angle between the two lines is:

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer: "30 degrees"}}
\]

First, I recognize that the given line is \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\). To find a parallel line passing through the point \((-4, 5)\), I need to use the same slope because parallel lines have equal slopes.

I'll rearrange the original equation into slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)) to identify its slope. 

Next, I'll substitute the coordinates of the given point into the general equation of a line with the same slope: \(y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)\), where \((x_1, y_1) = (-4, 5)\).

**Solution:**

To find the equation of a line parallel to the given line \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through the point \((-4, 5)\), follow these steps:

1. **Find the Slope of the Given Line:**
   
   Start by rewriting the given equation in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)) to identify its slope.

   \[
   5x - 6y + 4 = 0 \\
   -6y = -5x - 4 \\
   y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
   \]

   The slope (\(m\)) of the given line is \(\frac{5}{6}\).

2. **Use the Slope for the Parallel Line:**
   
   Since parallel lines have the same slope, the slope of the desired line is also \(\frac{5}{6}\).

3. **Find the Equation of the Parallel Line Using Point-Slope Form:**

   The point-slope form of a line is:

   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]

   Plugging in \(m = \frac{5}{6}\) and the point \((-4, 5)\):

   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
   \]

4. **Simplify to Standard Form:**
   
   Multiply both sides by 6 to eliminate the fraction:

   \[
   6(y - 5) = 5(x + 4) \\
   6y - 30 = 5x + 20 \\
   -5x + 6y = 50
   \]

   Rearranging terms gives the standard form of the equation:

   \[
   5x - 6y + (-50) = 0 \\
   \boxed{5x - 6y - 50 = 0}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{5x - 6y - 50 = 0}

To determine the distance between the two given parallel lines:

1. **Identify the Lines:**
   - First line: \( 19x + 12y - 38 = 0 \)
   - Second line: \( 38x + 24y + 70 = 0 \)

2. **Verify Parallelism:**
   - Both lines have the same slope.
     - Slope of first line: \( m = -\frac{19}{12} \)
     - Slope of second line: \( m = -\frac{38}{24} = -\frac{19}{12} \)

   Since the slopes are equal, the lines are **parallel**.

3. **Distance Between Parallel Lines:**
   The formula for the distance (\(d\)) between two parallel lines \( Ax + By + C_1 = 0 \) and \( Ax + By + C_2 = 0 \) is:
   \[
   d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
   \]

4. **Apply the Formula:**
   - Rewrite both lines in the form \( Ax + By + C = 0 \):
     - First line: \( 19x + 12y - 38 = 0 \) → \( C_1 = -38 \)
     - Second line: \( 38x + 24y + 70 = 0 \) can be simplified to:
       - Divide all terms by 2: \( 19x + 12y + 35 = 0 \) → \( C_2 = 35 \)
   - Calculate the distance:
     \[
     d = \frac{|-38 - 35|}{\sqrt{19^2 + 12^2}} = \frac{73}{\sqrt{361 + 144}} = \frac{73}{\sqrt{505}}
     \]

5. **Simplify the Distance:**
   - Rationalize or approximate if needed, but in exact form:
     \[
     d = \frac{73\sqrt{505}}{505}
     \]
   
However, upon closer examination, there's a mistake in the simplification step.

**Correction:**

- The correct formula should use \( C_1 - C_2 \), but since we've simplified the second line to have \( C = 35 \), let's adjust accordingly:
  
  The proper comparison is between \( 19x + 12y - 38 = 0 \) and \( 19x + 12y + 35 = 0 \).
  
- Thus, the correct calculation is:
  \[
  d = \frac{|-38 - (-35)|}{\sqrt{19^2 + 12^2}} = \frac{|-3|}{\sqrt{361 + 144}} = \frac{3}{\sqrt{505}}
  \]

**Final Answer:**

The distance between the two parallel lines is:
\[ 
\boxed{\dfrac{3}{\sqrt{505}}}

\]

**Solution:**

To find the equation of a line that is parallel to both \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\) and \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\), we can follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Direction Vector:**
   The direction vector of a line given by \(ax + by + c = 0\) is \((b, -a)\). For both lines:
   - Line 1 (\(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\)): Direction vector is \((6, -9)\).
   - Line 2 (\(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\)): Direction vector is \((2, -3)\).

   We can see that the direction vectors are scalar multiples of each other:
   \[
   (6, -9) = 3 \times (2, -3)
   \]
   Therefore, both lines have the same slope.

2. **Find a Line with the Same Slope:**
   Since we need a line parallel to both given lines, it must have the same slope. We can use either of the two given equations but change the constant term \(c\).

3. **Formulate the Equation:**
   Let's take the first equation and change its constant term:
   \[
   9x + 6y - k = 0
   \]
   Here, \(k\) is any real number.

4. **Alternative Form (Simplified):**
   Alternatively, we can simplify this by dividing all terms by 3 to get a simpler form:
   \[
   3x + 2y - c = 0
   \]
   Where \(c\) is any real number.

**Final Answer:**

There are infinitely many lines parallel to both given equations. They can be expressed as:

\[
\boxed{9x + 6y + k = 0}
\]

where \(k\) is any constant.
